Tahi response

Māori — Tangata Whenua, People of the Land

Māori are the indigenous people of New Zealand, known as tangata whenua — the people of the land. Their deep connection to this country is central to New Zealand's identity, history, and culture. 12

Origins and Early Settlement

The ancestors of Māori arrived by waka (canoe) from Pacific islands before 1300 CE, settling first along the coast where they hunted seals and moa, grew food, and gradually moved into the forests. 3 They lived in small tribal groups with a rich oral culture, strong traditions of warfare, and profound reverence for their ancestors and the gods of the natural world. 34

Māori tribes also carry a wealth of narratives about people and beings who existed long before the arrival of Polynesian settlers — collective knowledge that remains central to the experience and identity of tangata whenua today. 5

The bond between Māori and the land is captured in the expression "Ko au, ko te whenua, ko te whenua, ko au" — "I am the land and the land is me." 6

Traditional Society

Pre-European Māori society was oral and organised around three core units: whānau (family), hapū (sub-tribe), and iwi (tribe). 78 Society was also stratified into social rankings — rangatira (leaders), tūtūā (commoners), and taurekareka or mōkai (slaves) — with rank and leadership based on seniority of descent from founding ancestors. 9

Whakapapa (genealogy) was, and remains, central to Māori tradition. When Māori introduce themselves, they often reference the mountains, lakes, or rivers important to their tribe, grounding their identity firmly in the land. 1011

Kin relationships underpin many social customs. Hapū hold rights over particular marae (communal gathering places), hosting gatherings, weddings, and funerals. Kaumātua and kuia (male and female elders) hold an honoured place in communities. 12

The Arrival of Europeans and the Treaty of Waitangi

The arrival of Europeans from the early 1800s transformed Māori communities significantly. Missionaries arrived, many Māori converted to Christianity, and trade — particularly in pigs and potatoes — expanded rapidly. 3

In 1840, Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi) was signed between the British Crown and more than 500 Māori chiefs. 13 However, the English and Māori versions of the treaty carried different meanings — the English version gave the Crown sovereignty, while the Māori version guaranteed tino rangatiratanga (chieftainship) over their lands, villages, and treasured possessions. 1314 This difference in understanding eventually led to conflict, land loss, and a long process of reinterpretation that continues today. 15

Following the New Zealand Wars of the 1840s and 1860s, Māori lost vast amounts of land through confiscation and sale. 316

Revival and the 20th Century

In the first half of the 20th century, important leaders such as Apirana Ngata and Te Puea Hērangi worked to improve Māori wellbeing and revive the culture, renewing interest in the language and arts such as carving and weaving. 3

After the Second World War, many Māori moved to cities for work — a major shift from the rural, tribally centred life of previous generations. 8 In the 1970s and 1980s, political activism intensified, with significant events including:

  • A land march down the North Island to Parliament in 1975
  • The creation of the Waitangi Tribunal (1975) to examine alleged breaches of the Treaty
  • The occupation of Bastion Point in Auckland by protesters in 1977–78 3

Treaty Settlements

The Waitangi Tribunal was set up in 1975 to hear claims about Crown breaches of the treaty, and from 1985 could consider historic claims back to 1840. 16 Settlement negotiations led to landmark agreements — including the Sealord fisheries agreement ($170 million) in 1992, and the Waikato Tainui settlement ($170 million) in 1995. Some settlements also included formal Crown apologies and cultural redress such as the restoration of Māori place names — for example, Aoraki/Mt Cook. 16

Māori Today

By 2019, nearly 800,000 Māori people were living in New Zealand, with most in cities. The cultural landscape includes kōhanga reo (preschool language nests), Māori-language schools, two Māori television channels, 21 radio stations, and 29 members of Parliament who identified as Māori. 3

Te reo Māori became an official language of New Zealand in 1987, and a national target of one million te reo speakers by 2040 has been set. 17 The language's revitalisation has been driven by the kōhanga reo generation — young, fluent speakers who, in the words of Māori Language Commission chair Rawinia Higgins, "don't know what it means to not have te reo Māori." 18

From protest to celebration, Māori culture, identity, and language continue to grow and assert their place at the heart of Aotearoa New Zealand. 1819

Would you like to explore a particular aspect of Māori culture or history in more depth — such as te reo Māori, the Treaty settlement process, or traditional society?

Tahi response

Pacific Communities in Tāmaki Makaurau Auckland

Auckland is home to the largest concentration of Pacific people anywhere in Aotearoa — and indeed, anywhere in the world. With more than 275,000 Pacific people calling the city home, it is a place where Polynesian culture, identity and community life thrive in a uniquely New Zealand context. 1

Where Pacific Communities Settled

West and South Auckland have the largest Pacific and Māori communities, while the central city became home to earlier waves of migrants in suburbs like Ponsonby and Grey Lynn. 2 South Auckland, in particular, has more Pacific Islanders living there than any other urban centre in New Zealand. 3 From the 1970s onward, people from across the Pacific settled mainly in the Auckland area, forming communities where they could continue to speak their languages and maintain their customs. 4

In earlier decades, the inner-city suburbs of Ponsonby and Grey Lynn were at the heart of the Pasifika diaspora, drawing migrants who had come to New Zealand for work in the booming post-war manufacturing sector. 5

Who Makes Up the Community

The Pacific population of Auckland — and New Zealand more broadly — is richly diverse. The six largest groups, as of 2018, were Sāmoans, Tongans, Cook Islanders, Fijians, Niueans and Tokelauans. 6 But the community spans far further, with people from Kiribati, Tuvalu, Tahiti, Papua New Guinea, the Solomon Islands, and many other islands also represented. 4

As Pacific studies lecturer Melani Anae noted in 2001, there is no single 'Pacific community' — rather, Pacific peoples align themselves "variously, and at different times, along ethnic, geographic, church, family, school, age/gender, youth/elders, island born/NZ born, or occupational lines, or a mix of these." 7

A Distinct New Zealand Pacific Identity

Over time, something genuinely new has emerged in South Auckland: a distinctive New Zealand Pacific culture that is more than the sum of its parts. 8 Young Pacific people — known variously as PIs, Polys, or New Zealand borns — have developed new music, fashion, customs and ways of speaking, sometimes referred to collectively as Pasifika Aotearoa. 9

Auckland's music scene reflects this creative energy. A strong hip hop culture, first channelled through American Samoa in the 1980s, was revitalised by second and third generation Pacific Islanders in central and South Auckland. Artists such as Che Fu and Nesian Mystik, and the recording label Dawn Raid, have had a lasting influence on mainstream New Zealand music. 10

Pacific dance has also flourished in Auckland. By the 2000s, the city's Pacific dance community was so large and active it was drawing visitors from around the Pacific. Events like the Pasifika Festival and Polyfest have become major drawcards. 11

The Pasifika Festival

One of the most visible expressions of Pacific identity in Auckland is the annual Pasifika Festival at Western Springs. First held in 1993, it has grown into one of the largest celebrations of Pacific culture in the world, showcasing food, music, dance and art from 11 Pacific nations including Sāmoa, Tonga, Fiji, Niue, Tuvalu, the Cook Islands and Aotearoa. 112

The festival has faced significant challenges over the years — including cancellation following the Christchurch mosque attacks in 2019, pandemic disruptions in 2020, and the aftermath of devastating Auckland floods in 2023 — but has continued to grow and evolve. Festival director Jep Savali has said authenticity is now central to the festival's future, from food offerings to cultural performances. 13

A Growing and Young Population

The Pacific population across New Zealand has grown at roughly twice the rate of the overall population. By the 2023 census, Pacific peoples made up 8.9 percent of New Zealand's population — up from 8.1 percent in 2018 — with a median age of just 25, making it a notably young demographic. 14 Former Pacific peoples minister Aupito William Sio has described this growth as confirmation that "Pasifika as well as Māori are the future of New Zealand." 14

Would you like to explore more about the cultural celebrations and festivals that bring Auckland's Pacific communities together?

Tahi response

Kia Ora — Aotearoa's Beloved Greeting

The most widely used te reo Māori greeting is 'kia ora', which functions as a general hello. It's beautifully versatile — both Māori and Pākehā New Zealanders use it in everyday life, and it's increasingly common across all settings, from casual encounters to formal occasions. 1

When saying farewell, you'd say 'ka kite' or 'ka kite anō' — the te reo equivalent of 'see you' or 'see you again'. 1 Another greeting worth knowing is 'tēnā koe', which is also used as a way of acknowledging the person in front of you. 2

Physical Greetings

Beyond words, Māori greeting customs involve a physical element that carries deep cultural meaning. The hongi — the ceremonial pressing of noses — is one of the most distinctive and ancient of these customs. 3

In a formal Māori context, you would shake hands and hongi (press noses briefly) when meeting someone. 4 The hongi connects back to the mythological figure of Tāne Mahuta, who breathed life into the first woman, Hineahuone ('woman formed of earth'), through the nose — making the sharing of breath a deeply symbolic act of peace and connection. 3

A Language of Welcome

The phrase 'haere mai' — meaning 'welcome' — is also a cornerstone of Māori greeting tradition, historically called out to approaching visitors to invite them in. 56 Formal welcomes, known as pōwhiri, can include the karanga (a ceremonial call by women), whaikōrero (formal speeches), and the hongi, all following a pattern that dates back to before European arrival in Aotearoa. 73

The Māori Language Commission (Te Taura Whiri i te Reo Māori) encourages all New Zealanders — Māori and non-Māori alike — to embrace simple greetings like 'kia ora' and 'tēnā koe' as a meaningful way to support and celebrate te reo Māori. As their tumu whakahaere (chief executive) Ngahiwi Apanui Barr put it, "whether it's speaking Māori every day... or just using greetings like kia ora or tēnā koe, it all adds up to te reo Māori surviving and being valued." 2

Would you like to know more about the pōwhiri ceremony and what to expect if you're welcomed onto a marae?

Tahi response

Te Ika a Māui — The Fish of Māui

The Māori name for the North Island is Te Ika a Māui, meaning 'the fish of Māui' (or 'Māui's fish'). 12 It comes from one of the great stories of Māori tradition — the tale of the demigod Māui fishing up the land from the sea.

The Story Behind the Name

According to tradition, Māui and his brothers set out on a fishing expedition. His elder brothers didn't want him along, so he hid in the canoe and only revealed himself once they were far out at sea. He persuaded his brothers to row to the deepest part of the ocean, where he cast a fish hook made from his grandmother's jawbone. It sank beneath the waves and fastened to the underwater house of Tonganui, grandson of Tangaroa, the god of the sea — and Māui hauled the land itself up from the depths. 3

The North Island is said to take the form of a giant stingray, with North Cape known as Te Hiku o Te Ika (the tail of the fish) and Wellington as Te Ūpoko o Te Ika (the head of the fish). 4

What Explorer James Cook Heard

When James Cook asked Māori the name of the North Island, he recorded it as 'Eaheinomauwe' — possibly a mishearing of either He Mea hī nō Māui (the things Māui fished up) or Te Ahi a Māui (the fires of Māui), the latter referring to both Māui having brought fire to the world and the volcanic nature of much of the island. 1

The Wider Picture

The name fits into a broader tradition connecting the islands of New Zealand to Māui's voyage. The South Island is Te Waka a Māui (Māui's canoe, from which he fished), and Stewart Island/Rakiura is Te Punga a Māui (Māui's anchor stone). 12

It's worth noting that while Te Ika a Māui is the most widely known name, the North Island was also sometimes referred to as Aotearoa — a name now used to refer to all of New Zealand. 5

Would you like to know more about the legend of Māui and how it shapes other place names around Aotearoa?

Tahi response

Ka Mate — The Famous Haka of Ngāti Toa Rangatira

'Ka Mate' is one of Aotearoa New Zealand's most celebrated haka — a powerful chant with movement whose opening words, 'Ka mate, ka mate, ka ora, ka ora', translate roughly as 'It is death, it is death, it is life, it is life.' The haka speaks to the triumph of life over death, and its themes of survival and resilience remain as resonant today as when it was first composed. 1

Who Composed It and When?

Ka Mate was composed by Te Rauparaha, the great rangatira (chief) of Ngāti Toa Rangatira (also known as Ngāti Toa), around 1820. 2 It is said to have been first composed at Lake Rotoaira, in the central North Island. 2

Which Iwi Does It Belong To?

Ka Mate belongs to Ngāti Toa Rangatira, an iwi whose lands are largely in the south-western North Island, centred around Porirua and the Kāpiti Coast. 3 The significance of Ka Mate to Ngāti Toa's history, culture and identity was formally recognised through special legislation, passed as part of the iwi's Treaty of Waitangi settlement signed on 7 December 2012. 1

That legislation requires acknowledgement of Te Rauparaha as the composer of Ka Mate in certain circumstances — a significant cultural and legal recognition of Ngāti Toa's intellectual and ancestral connection to the haka. 1

Ka Mate Today

The haka continues to be a living expression of Ngāti Toa identity. In June 2025, Ngāti Toa Rangatira led the hautapu (ceremonial offering) at Matariki, and the origins and significance of Ka Mate were discussed publicly by Kahu o Te Rangi Ropata, the iwi's pou tikanga reo (resident expert in Māori cultural matters). 2

Would you like to know more about the Treaty settlement that formally recognised Ngāti Toa's ownership of Ka Mate?

Tahi response

Tangata Whenua of the Wellington Region

The tangata whenua (people of the land) of the Wellington region are primarily the Taranaki tribes who migrated south in the early 19th century. The three key iwi (tribes) are:

  • Te Āti Awa
  • Ngāti Mutunga
  • Ngāti Tama

1

The Story of Migration

These iwi originally came from Taranaki and were pushed southward due to raids by northern tribes, particularly the Waikato people. Ngāti Toa were also displaced from Kāwhia by the same Waikato incursions. 2

Te Āti Awa, the most prominent of the Wellington tribes, moved south in four migrations, or heke, between roughly 1822 and 1834:

  1. Te Heke Tātaramoa — about 1822
  2. Te Heke Nihoputa — about 1824
  3. Te Heke Tamateuaua — 1832
  4. Te Heke Paukena — 1834

2

What Happened Next

By 1835, when Ngāti Mutunga and some Ngāti Tama migrated further to the Chatham Islands, their lands around the harbour were passed to Te Āti Awa and other Taranaki chiefs. 2 Then, from 1839 onwards, European land purchases and settlement forced Māori to relocate outside the new town. Many returned to Taranaki, though after World War I they began moving back to Wellington to find work. 2

Te Āti Awa Today

In 1977, the Wellington Tenths Trust was established to represent the descendants of Te Āti Awa and other Taranaki people who had lived in the Wellington area before European settlement. It administers the lands set aside for Māori in the original Wellington land purchases. In the 2013 census, more than 2,500 people identified as descended from Te Āti Awa of Wellington. 2

A symbol of these deep Taranaki roots can even be seen at Wellington's waterfront stadium, where a large sculpture of Mt Taranaki forms an archway over the entrance. 1

    https://teara.govt.nz/en/te-ati-awa-of-wellington

Tahi response

Tonga and Samoa: A Tale of Two Cultures

Both Tonga and Samoa are deeply rooted Pacific Island cultures with rich traditions, and while they share some broad similarities — strong Christian faith, reverence for family, and a proud sense of identity — each has its own distinct cultural framework.

The Heart of Tongan Culture: Anga Faka Tonga

At the core of Tongan identity is anga faka Tonga — literally 'the Tongan way'. This encompasses values of sharing, respect, and obedience, all of which are upheld especially within the extended family. 1

A particularly distinctive Tongan tradition is the cultural authority held by a father's sisters over their nieces and nephews. This role is considered deeply honourable, even if younger Tongans raised outside Tonga sometimes find it confusing at first. 2

Tongan culture also observes very strict gender roles. Girls are raised to be home-oriented and submissive, while boys are reared to be independent and mobile. Interestingly, while men hold more political power, women are ranked higher in social standing within the community. 3

Tongan dance traditions are also richly ceremonial. Before performing, young unmarried women traditionally have their bodies oiled with coconut oil — often sent from family in Tonga, perfumed with local leaves. A further tradition sees audiences show appreciation by pressing bank notes onto the oiled skin of performers. 4

The Heart of Samoan Culture: Fa'asamoa

Samoan identity is anchored in the concept of fa'asamoa — the Samoan way — which encompasses a web of values and traditions: 5

  • Aiga (family) — the central pillar of Samoan life 6
  • Tautala Samoa — the Samoan language 5
  • Gafa — genealogies 5
  • Matai — the chiefly system 5
  • Lotu — the church 5
  • Fa'alavelave — ceremonial and family obligations 5

Associated values include alofa (love), tautua (service), fa'aaloalo (respect), feagaiga (a covenant between siblings and others), and usita'i (discipline). 5

Within the aiga, young people are expected to serve and respect their elders, and in return receive love, protection, honour, and support. 6 Generosity is not optional — family, church, and village all make constant demands on individuals, and one's social standing is closely tied to how much one gives. 7

Sunday is treated as a day of enforced rest in Samoa — reserved for eating, sleeping, and church — with activities like sport or even swimming off the table. 8

Shared Threads

Both cultures share some striking commonalities:

  • Christianity plays a central role in both Tongan and Samoan community life, with large and prominent churches being a feature of both cultures. 910
  • Extended family is the bedrock of both societies, with obligations extending across generations and across the globe. 111
  • Respect for elders is a core value in both traditions, with younger generations expected to serve and defer to those older. 16

Identity in a Changing World

Both communities, particularly those living in New Zealand, are navigating how to carry their traditions into new environments. Tongan New Zealanders have emerged in several broad types — those raised fully in anga faka Tonga, those born in New Zealand with decreasing language fluency, and very few who have fully assimilated. 12 Similarly, for Samoans, fa'asamoa remains constant even as its expression shifts — with the aiga (family) described as 'a permanent fixture' even as friendships and other ties come and go. 11

Both cultures have also shown a remarkable ability to enrich New Zealand society through sport, music, art, and community leadership. 1310

Would you like to explore how either of these communities has maintained their cultural traditions while living in New Zealand?

Tahi response

Māori and the New Zealand Justice System

The relationship between Māori and the justice system in Aotearoa New Zealand is one of the country's most significant and complex social issues — spanning centuries of legal history, deep structural inequities, and ongoing debate about how to create a fairer system for all New Zealanders.

From Tikanga to British Law

Before 1840, the law of the land was tikanga Māori — the customs and methods of governance by which Māori society was ordered. This system was built around concepts such as mana (status, either inherited or acquired), tapu (sacred prohibition), rahui (a form of tapu restricting access to certain food sources), utu (repayment for another's actions), and muru (a ritual seizure of property as compensation for an offence). 1

When the Treaty of Waitangi was signed in 1840, British law arrived in force. In practice, however, it only applied in areas of significant Pākehā settlement; elsewhere, Māori traditional systems of governance continued to predominate. 2

Māori customary law is still recognised in New Zealand today, provided certain criteria are met: the custom must be longstanding, have continued without interruption, be reasonable, be certain in its terms, and must not have been displaced by Parliament through clear statutory wording. 3

Specialist Courts and Institutions

The New Zealand court system includes several institutions specifically designed to address Māori needs and rights.

The Māori Land Court is the oldest specialist court in the country, having dealt with the alienation of Māori land since 1865 — when it was called the Native Land Court. It rules on issues of multiple ownership of Māori land, with appeals going to the Māori Appellate Court. 4

The Waitangi Tribunal, established in 1975, is a unique institution where Māori can bring complaints about breaches of the Treaty of Waitangi. From 1985, it was empowered to investigate breaches dating back to 1840. It makes recommendations to the government, and on the basis of its findings, the Crown has undertaken a range of treaty settlements since the late 1980s — including the restoration of fisheries to Māori ownership in 1992. 35

Māori Over-Representation in Prison

Perhaps the most confronting dimension of this topic is the dramatic over-representation of Māori in the prison system. In the 19th century, very few Māori were imprisoned — usually less than 3% of the total prison population. 2 That began to change in the early 20th century, with Māori urbanisation — accelerated by the Second World War — a clear contributing factor. By 1945, Māori made up 21% of prison admissions while representing just 6% of the total population. 6

By 1971, Māori were 40% of the prison population while forming around 10% of the country's total population. Since 1980, Māori have consistently made up about half of all prisoners. 7

More recent data paints an even starker picture. A 2025 study found Māori were overrepresented at every stage of the criminal justice system — 37% of people proceeded against by police, 45% of those convicted, and over half of the prison population at 52%, despite Māori making up only 17.8% of the population. In women's prisons, the proportion rises to 61–63%. 8

The same research found Māori were being undercounted in prison data by around 6% — roughly 405 people — because Corrections was not following national ethnicity recording protocols. Lead researcher Paula King said this affected resource allocation and government policy decisions: "If you're invisibilising Māori, you can't monitor Crown actions or inactions, or accurately assess the impact of policies." 8

Sentencing Disparities

The inequities don't stop at the prison gate. A 2025 study examining first-time drink-driving cases found that, despite having similar alcohol levels at the time of offending, 9.9% of Māori offenders received a community-based sentence compared with just 3.3% of NZ Europeans. Even after controlling for offence characteristics and a range of demographic and socioeconomic factors, Māori remained twice as likely as NZ Europeans to receive a community-based sentence — a more severe outcome than a fine. 9

Separate analysis from the Understanding Policing Delivery programme found systemic bias at the policing stage: when all other factors were held constant, Māori were 11% more likely to be prosecuted than Pākehā. 9

Contributing Factors

The reasons behind the high rates of Māori imprisonment are widely acknowledged to be complex. They include the larger proportion of Māori in lower socioeconomic groups, a younger age profile in the Māori population, higher rates of unemployment, the ongoing effects of urbanisation, and the influence of gang culture. Some critics argue the justice system disadvantages Māori at every stage — from arrest through to sentencing. 7

Those released from prison face dire outcomes. The mortality rate of people released from prison is three times that of the general population, with the first month after release the most dangerous. Mortality is worse for wāhine Māori (Māori women). The research also found that only 76% of Māori released from prison were enrolled with a Primary Health Organisation, leaving a quarter without access to subsidised care. 8

Responses and Reforms

Several initiatives have sought to address these realities. The Department of Corrections runs Māori Focus Units, open to medium and minimum security prisoners, which aim to change behaviour through a greater understanding of tikanga Māori. The department also has a policy of consulting with Māori community groups and recruiting more Māori staff. 10

At a community level, marae-based programmes — such as those run by Tūhoe Hauora in Tāneatua — have proved successful for rangatahi (young people) who come into conflict with the law, using tikanga-based approaches to build self-worth and a sense of direction. 11

However, critics — including nurses working inside prisons — raised concerns in 2024 when the coalition government moved to remove Te Tiriti o Waitangi provisions from the Corrections Amendment Bill. Those provisions had compelled the Department of Corrections to improve outcomes for Māori and maintain obligations around healthcare and rehabilitation programmes. The New Zealand Nurses Organisation (NZNO) called the move "deeply shameful." 12

Meanwhile, sentencing reforms passed in 2025 — which cap the discounts judges can apply for mitigating factors — have drawn criticism for their likely disproportionate impact on Māori, given Māori already make up the majority of the prison population. The Ministry of Justice's own regulatory impact statement noted that the changes rested on inadequate consultation, particularly with Māori. 13

The debate continues about the right path forward, with researchers, community leaders, and whānau (families) consistently pointing to the need for kaupapa Māori (Māori-centred) approaches, secure housing, employment pathways, and sustained community support after release. As one researcher put it: "We're trying to break cycles of harm for future generations, to create a world our mokopuna (grandchildren) can live well in." 8

Would you like to explore more about the Waitangi Tribunal and how Treaty of Waitangi settlements have shaped Māori rights in New Zealand?

Tahi response

Whakapapa — The Foundation of Māori Knowledge

At its most fundamental, whakapapa means genealogy — a line of descent tracing ancestors down to the present day. 1 But it is far more than a family tree. Whakapapa is a taxonomic framework that links all animate and inanimate, known and unknown phenomena in both the terrestrial and spiritual worlds. 2 It is, in the words of East Coast elder Apirana Ngata, 'the process of laying one thing upon another' — visualising foundation ancestors as a first generation, with each succeeding generation placed upon them in ordered layers. 2

Whakapapa forms the very core of traditional mātauranga Māori (Māori knowledge), and provides a way of understanding the universe — its past, present and future. 3 Experts recited the whakapapa of people, birds, fish, trees and even the weather to explain relationships between all things, and to understand how to act within those relationships. 4

Whakapapa and the Origins of Everything

Whakapapa doesn't begin with humans — it reaches back to the very origins of the universe. Creation stories vary from tribe to tribe, but many trace descent from the gods themselves. In one tradition, Ranginui (the sky father) and Papatūānuku (the earth mother) gave birth to gods such as Tangaroa, god of the sea, and Tāne Mahuta, god of the forest — and people could trace their own whakapapa back to these atua (gods). 1

Whakapapa also explains the origins of animals, plants and features of the landscape. To tell the origin story of a bird, for example, is to invoke its true essence or character. 5 Some tribal groups even claim descent from Tangaroa alongside the fish of the sea — expressing a profound kinship with the natural world. 5

Many of these traditions are shared across the Pacific, with names like Māui and Whiro (known as Hiro in Hawaiʻi) and similar stories appearing across Polynesia, reflecting deep ancestral connections. 1

How Whakapapa Is Used

Whakapapa functions as an organising principle across many areas of Māori life:

Identity and belonging: It is through whakapapa that individuals receive their names, their sense of identity, their turangawaewae (place to stand on ancestral land), and their access to knowledge, rights and responsibilities. 6 As one source puts it, whakapapa places people 'in the whole context of relationships and therefore how we relate to each other and how we should work with each other, argue with each other, live with each other.' 6

Land and rights: Reciting whakapapa was the way Māori proved their connections to land when appearing before the Native Land Court to establish title. 1

Preserving knowledge: Whakapapa maps relationships so that mythology, legend, history, tikanga (custom), philosophies and spiritualities are organised, preserved and transmitted from one generation to the next. 2

Reconnection: Today, knowledge of whakapapa remains deeply meaningful. Many Māori who have felt disconnected from their heritage — particularly as a result of urbanisation policies that scattered whānau — are finding that researching their whakapapa is a transformative act of reconnection. 76

Precision health: Researchers are even exploring how whakapapa data can be combined with genomic science to provide more accurate, personalised health treatment for Māori — recognising that whakapapa is, in one view, the cultural counterpart to DNA. 8

How Whakapapa Is Passed On

Traditionally, whakapapa was transmitted orally. The most common form is tararere, which gives a single line of descent from an ancestor. A shorter form giving just the names of key ancestors is known as tātai hikohiko or āhua hikohiko. 1

Whakapapa were recalled through waiata (songs), particularly oriori sung to children, and through stories connecting ancestry to the landscape — some experts could recite hundreds of names from memory. 1 Specialists used rākau whakapapa, notched sticks held during recitation, moving their hands along the notches as each name was spoken. 1

When writing arrived, some whakapapa were recorded in manuscripts considered so tapu (sacred) that when their authors died, the books were sometimes burnt or buried with them. 1

The wharenui (meeting house) itself can be read as a physical model of whakapapa — the oldest ancestors sit at the apex near the tekoteko (carved figure), with lines spreading outward and downward to the most recent generations. 1

It's worth noting that whakapapa is considered deeply tapu, and perspectives on its use and transmission can vary significantly between iwi and hapū — what is shared here is general context, not a definitive account for any particular people.

Would you like to explore how whakapapa connects to a specific area, such as the natural world, land rights, or its role in modern health research?

Tahi response

Tapu and Noa — Sacred Balance in te ao Māori

Tapu and noa are two of the most fundamental concepts in te ao Māori (the Māori world view), representing a sacred tension that shapes relationships between people, the natural world, and the spiritual realm.

Tapu — The Spiritual Essence of All Things

Tapu is far more than a simple prohibition or 'keep out' sign. It is best understood as the spiritual essence that resides in all living things and objects. 1 It arises from mauri — the life principle of all creation — and connects everything back to its ultimate source. 1

Some key expressions of tapu include:

  • In nature: Trees, rocks, mountains, fish, and birds all carry tapu. For example, the mountain Tongariro is considered so tapu that some Māori will not even look at it. 1
  • In people: The highest expression of tapu is found in human beings. Even a common greeting like 'Kia ora' — literally 'have life!' — is, at a deeper level, an acknowledgement of another person's tapu and inherent dignity. 1
  • In language: Te reo Māori itself is considered tapu, because it expresses the very soul of a person. Karakia (prayers and incantations) that enrich every aspect of life — from birth to death — depend on language to be effective. 2
  • In special places: Shrines (tūāhu) and sacred places (wāhi tapu), such as burial grounds, carry strong tapu. 3

Importantly, Māori belief holds that the natural and supernatural worlds are inseparable — there was no Māori word for 'religion' before European arrival, because spirituality was simply the entire way of being. 34

Noa — The Ordinary, the Everyday

Noa is the counterpart to tapu — it describes the state of being ordinary, free from restriction, and safe for everyday interaction. 3 While tapu elevates and restricts, noa neutralises and releases.

Ceremonies could be performed to remove tapu from a person, place, or object and return it to a noa state. 3 A practical, everyday example: eating and drinking are considered noa activities, which is why they are forbidden within an urupā (cemetery), which is highly tapu. Washing hands with water after leaving an urupā is a customary way of lifting tapu and restoring a noa state. 5

When the Balance is Disrupted

Keeping tapu and noa properly separated matters deeply in tikanga (cultural values and practice). When state housing policies in the 20th century aimed at assimilating Māori into Pākehā society, they paid no attention to this distinction — for instance, placing a washing machine next to a kitchen sink, which mixed tapu and noa spaces. 6 Such violations are not taken lightly; they affect not just the individual but the wider whānau and iwi. 2

Mana and the Marae

The marae welcomes this dynamic beautifully. When visitors (manuhiri) approach, they exist in a state of tapu — unknown and potentially carrying their own spiritual weight. The process of pōwhiri (welcome) works through this tension: through call and response, kōrero (speech), waiata (song), and finally the hongi (pressing of noses), the tapu is lifted and visitors become hunga kāinga — people of the house, now sharing in the mana and protection of the tangata whenua (people of the land). 1

It's worth noting that tapu and noa can vary in expression and application across different iwi and hapū — the perspectives shared here are general, and specific communities are the authorities on their own tikanga.

Would you like to explore how tapu relates to the concept of mana (prestige and authority) in more depth?

Tahi response

Tuna and the Waterways of Aotearoa

The relationship between tuna and New Zealand's rivers, lakes and wetlands, as understood through mātauranga Māori (Māori traditional knowledge), is profound — stretching from the heavens to the sea, and from the very origins of the natural world to the dinner table and beyond.

Tuna in Creation and Tradition

Mātauranga Māori explains the origins of tuna through several traditions. In one account, the ancestors of the freshwater eel — alongside frostfish, conger eels and hagfish — came from Puna kauariki, a spring in the highest heavens. When the waters of the heavens dried up, this group made their way down to Papatūānuku (the earth). Tuna remained in fresh water, while the others went to the sea 1.

In another well-known tradition, a giant eel called Tuna frightened the wives of the demigod Māui. As punishment, Māui cut Tuna in half — one half fell into the sea and became the conger eel, while the other landed in a river and became the freshwater eel 21. These stories are not simply mythology; they encode a deep understanding of the connections between fresh and salt water, and of tuna as a being with celestial origins.

More Than a Fish — An Ancestor

For many iwi, tuna are not merely a food source; they are ancestors, kin, and the embodiment of identity. As one voice from Ngāti Manawa puts it, tuna are "intrinsically linked to our arrival and our being... The species is an ancestor to us, a brother to us. It's a mother to us and the livelihood of our people" 3.

This ancestral relationship is woven into waiata (song) and karakia (prayer). At the Ngāti Manawa meeting house in Murupara, eight kaitiaki (guardians) in the form of eels adorn the carvings — and the town itself is named after one of them. Kaumātua (elders) Maurice Toetoe notes that Ngāti Manawa's waiata and karakia tell of the eels' migration to their tropical Pacific spawning ground, and that the old people did not need Western science to understand this: the knowledge was already theirs 4.

Māori had more than 70 names for eels, depending on species, colouration and other characteristics — a rich taxonomic vocabulary that speaks to centuries of intimate observation 5.

Tuna as Taonga (Treasure)

Tuna are explicitly understood as taonga — treasures — within te ao Māori (the Māori world). Rivers and lakes where eels were plentiful were, and remain, deeply valued. The Mōhaka River in Hawke's Bay, for instance, has long been an important source of food, particularly eels, for Māori, and remains a taonga for Ngāti Pahauwera hapū (subtribe) to this day 6.

For generations, Māori connection to rivers, lakes and wetlands was forged through the harvesting of eels 7. Tuna were a cornerstone of Māori society — providing readily available protein that could be preserved and stored, or traded for other commodities 5.

Knowledge of Migration and the Lifecycle

Mātauranga Māori demonstrates a sophisticated understanding of tuna migration. Every year, eels swim out to sea to breed, and Māori regularly harvested them during this tuna heke (eel migration). Techniques included pā tuna (eel weirs built as fences in rivers), hīnaki (woven eel pots), torchlight fishing, and digging drains to intercept migrating eels at places like Wairewa/Lake Forsyth, where this practice has been maintained for centuries 28.

At Wairewa, mana whenua groups have dug channels through gravel bars to help tuna complete their migration to the sea — a practice that fuses ancient knowledge with active environmental stewardship 9.

Kaitiakitanga and the Health of Waterways

Central to mātauranga Māori is the principle of kaitiakitanga — guardianship and protection of the environment. Rivers, lakes and wetlands carry mauri (life force), and it is the responsibility of kaitiaki to preserve it 10. Incorporating mātauranga Māori into freshwater management, advocates argue, is crucial to meeting the challenges facing freshwater ecosystems: "We know the lay of the land. We understand what it's trying to tell us" 11.

The river itself, in the Māori world view, is not a resource to be used but a living ancestor. The phrase common to those on the Whanganui River — "Ko au te awa, ko te awa ko au" ("I am the river, the river is me") — captures this entirely 12. When rivers are degraded, it is experienced as a personal and spiritual affront: "The river is our ancestor. You don't treat your ancestor like that," says Ngāti Manawa kaumātua Toetoe of the Rangitāiki 13.

Mātauranga as Living Knowledge

This knowledge is not static — it is actively practised and transmitted. Kaumātua such as Brent Ruru of Kāti Irakehu and Kāti Mako continue to teach the processing, smoking and drying of tuna, insisting: "We need to keep the tradition going. Otherwise we can't go out and gather our own kai. What's left for the people? What's left for the young ones?" 4

The concern is real: knowledge can be lost in just a generation 13. As tuna populations decline due to dams, drained wetlands and degraded waterways, so too is there an urgency to maintain and apply the mātauranga that sustained both people and ecosystems for centuries 14.

Would you like to explore how mātauranga Māori is being applied in contemporary freshwater management and conservation efforts for tuna today?

Tahi response

My search results offer only limited direct insight into Māori perspectives on offshore oil and gas exploration specifically. However, a few relevant threads do emerge.

Kaitiakitanga and the Environment

At a foundational level, many Māori perspectives on resource extraction are shaped by the concept of kaitiakitanga — guardianship and protection of the natural world. In the Māori world view, people are deeply connected to the land and sea, and the mauri (life force) of oceans, rivers and forests must be protected. 1 This cultural framework often places Māori communities in tension with large-scale extractive industries.

Ngāti Ruanui and Seabed Mining

One concrete example from the search results involves Ngāti Ruanui, an iwi (tribe) in Taranaki, which has actively opposed seabed mining applications in the South Taranaki Bight from the outset. Regional Development Minister Shane Jones described this opposition as 'cultural wind' — a dismissive remark that drew attention to the friction between Māori communities and proponents of offshore resource development in the region. 2

Shane Jones: A Māori Voice For the Industry

It's worth noting that Jones himself is Māori, and has been one of the most outspoken advocates for reviving the offshore oil and gas sector. He described the previous government's offshore exploration ban as 'the worst political decision' he had been involved with, and has been actively working to attract new investment back to New Zealand. 2 This illustrates that Māori opinion on the issue is not monolithic — perspectives vary significantly across individuals and iwi.

What the Search Results Don't Cover

The available results don't provide a comprehensive survey of iwi or hapū (subtribe) views on offshore oil and gas exploration more broadly. Māori perspectives on this issue are likely diverse, shaped by factors including proximity to affected areas, Treaty of Waitangi rights, economic interests, and tikanga (customary values) around environmental stewardship.

Tahi response

Why Are Māori So Overrepresented in New Zealand's Prisons?

The overrepresentation of Māori in New Zealand's prison system is one of the country's most persistent and troubling social issues — and the causes are complex, deeply rooted in history, and span multiple interconnected systems. While Māori make up around 17.8% of New Zealand's population, they account for 52% of the prison population, and in women's prisons the proportion rises to 61–63% 12.

Researchers, advocates and the justice system itself acknowledge there is no single explanation. Instead, a web of historical, socioeconomic, and systemic factors all play a role.

The Legacy of Colonisation

Perhaps the most foundational cause is colonisation itself. The arrival of British settlers brought war, disease, land confiscation, and legislative policies designed to assimilate Māori into Pākehā (European New Zealander) society — often through force 34.

The Native Schools Act 1867, for instance, established an education system that banned te reo Māori (the Māori language) in schools, enforced by severe corporal punishment. Over generations, this disconnected whānau (families) from their language, culture, and identity 4. As Dr Rawiri Waretini Karena has argued, "You can't separate abuse in state care from our history of colonisation and indigenous oppression" 4.

This colonial disruption contributed directly to what researchers describe as a "state care to prison pipeline." When Waretini Karena first entered prison as a teenager, he already knew 80% of the men there — they had grown up together in state care. From 1950 to 1999, more than 200,000 children were subject to abuse in state care, and Māori children were disproportionately funnelled into that system 4.

Socioeconomic Disadvantage

Colonisation created lasting inequalities that persist today. Māori are disadvantaged relative to most other groups in New Zealand across a range of measures — lower life expectancy, lower living and housing standards, poorer health outcomes, and lower educational attainment 5.

Economic reforms in the late 1980s and 1990s rapidly increased inequality and greatly affected the most vulnerable communities, many of which were Māori 6. Higher rates of unemployment, a younger population demographic, lower socioeconomic status, and the impact of urbanisation have all been identified as contributing factors to higher rates of offending and, in turn, imprisonment 7.

Systemic Bias at Every Stage of the Justice System

A critical finding from recent research is that Māori are overrepresented not just in prisons, but at every stage of the criminal justice system:

  • 37% of people proceeded against by police are Māori
  • 45% of those convicted are Māori
  • Over 52% of the prison population is Māori 12

Research from the Understanding Policing Delivery programme found that, with all other factors held constant, Māori were 11% more likely to be prosecuted than Pākehā 28. Māori men are also more likely to be stopped, tasered, and prosecuted, with studies attributing this to bias and structural racism in policing 9.

Sentencing disparities compound the problem further. A study examining first-time drink-driving offences found that despite having similar alcohol levels, 9.9% of Māori offenders received a community-based sentence compared with just 3.3% of New Zealand Europeans. Even after controlling for demographic and socioeconomic factors, Māori were twice as likely to receive a harsher sentence 2.

The Urbanisation Factor

The dramatic rise in Māori imprisonment through the 20th century closely tracked urbanisation. Māori urbanisation, accelerated by the Second World War, severed many Māori from their land, communities, and support structures (iwi and hapū networks). By 1945, Māori made up 21% of prison admissions despite being just 6% of the total population 10. By 1971, they were 40% of the prison population 7.

Data Gaps That Mask the True Scale

A 2025 University of Otago study (the TIAKI project) found that Māori are actually undercounted in prison data by around 6%, or approximately 405 people, because Corrections was not following national ethnicity recording protocols. This undercounting has obscured the true scale of the issue and affected government resource allocation and policy decisions 1.

Associate Professor Paula King (Te Aupōuri, Te Rarawa, Ngāpuhi, Ngāti Whātua, Waikato Tainui, Ngāti Maniapoto), who led the research, put it plainly: "If you're invisibilising Māori, you can't monitor Crown actions or inactions, or accurately assess the impact of policies" 1.

Looking Forward

The TIAKI study found whānau released from prison consistently identified the same needs: secure housing, employment pathways, culturally grounded programmes, and sustained whanaungatanga-based (community relationship-based) support. King noted that Māori providers already offer much of this support, but have been underfunded for decades 1.

As one Māori woman prisoner observed: "Sticking us in jail ain't gonna do nothing... you take us away from the community and then when we get out we don't know what else to do... They're doing it all wrong thinking why their jails are filling up" 7.

The evidence is clear: addressing Māori overrepresentation in prisons requires confronting not just crime, but the colonial history, systemic bias, and deep socioeconomic inequality that feeds it.

Would you like to explore what specific solutions or programmes — such as Māori Focus Units in prisons or kaupapa Māori (Māori-centred) rehabilitation approaches — have been tried to address this issue?

Tahi response

That's a lovely intention for your team meeting! Unfortunately, my search results don't contain a specific karakia suitable for opening a workplace or team gathering. The results I have include a karakia used in traditional Māori martial arts to gather strength before a wrestling bout 1, and some contextual information about the nature and purpose of karakia more broadly — but nothing I'd be comfortable presenting as an appropriate opening karakia for a meeting setting.

What I can share is that karakia are described as a way of communicating with the gods, and as "a formula of words chanted to obtain benefit or avert trouble" 2. Their use in modern, non-traditional settings — including workplaces and public institutions — has become increasingly common in Aotearoa New Zealand as part of a genuine bicultural commitment 34. However, it's also worth noting that the cultural integrity of how karakia are used matters — they carry real spiritual and cultural weight, and it's worth approaching their use thoughtfully rather than tokenistically 3.

A Better Path Forward

For an appropriate karakia to open your team meeting, I'd recommend:

  • Asking a local kaumātua (elder) or someone with te reo Māori expertise in your organisation or community
  • Contacting your iwi (tribal) liaison or a local marae
  • Checking with your organisation's Māori cultural advisors, if you have them

This ensures the karakia you use is culturally appropriate, correctly pronounced, and meaningful — rather than a tick-box exercise 3.

Would you like to know more about the broader role of karakia in Māori cultural life and how they've been adapted for contemporary settings?

Tahi response

Two Peoples, One Whakapapa (Genealogy)

Cook Island Māori and New Zealand Māori are distinct peoples with separate cultural identities, languages, and histories — yet they share deep ancestral roots that connect them across the Pacific.

Origins and Shared Ancestry

The two groups are closely related by whakapapa. It is likely that some ancestors of New Zealand Māori arrived from the Cook Islands around 700 years ago. 1 Oral traditions from Aitutaki in the Cook Islands even claim that the famous Tainui waka (canoe) stopped there on its voyage from Ra'iatea to New Zealand, and Cook Islanders rekindled links with the Tainui iwi (tribe) in the 1990s. 2 The two peoples share place names, and even family names — for example, Paikea, which originated with a family from Mauke who settled near East Cape in New Zealand. 2

There is, however, a touch of friendly sibling rivalry in this relationship. As one journalist has noted, "there is a bit of stick between Cook Islands Maori and New Zealand Maori. One group maintains they kicked the other out a thousand years ago … The voyagers claim those who stayed behind were the slack ones!" 1

Different Homelands, Different Status

A key distinction is that New Zealand Māori are the tangata whenua — the people of the land — and are recognised as the original inhabitants of Aotearoa New Zealand. 3 Their history in New Zealand includes a rich oral culture, centuries of settlement, the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi in 1840, land wars, and a long struggle for cultural and political recognition. 4

Cook Island Māori, by contrast, are a Pacific community who originate from the Cook Islands — a self-governing nation in free association with New Zealand. Cook Islanders hold New Zealand citizenship but come from a separate homeland with its own governance. 5

Language: Related but Distinct

Both peoples speak Māori languages that fall under the same Tahitic branch of the Austronesian language family, which means the dialects of the Cook Islands are very similar to te reo Māori. 26 However, they are separate languages. Cook Islands Māori (te reo Māori Kūki 'Airani) has several island dialects, with Rarotongan emerging as the dominant form. 7

Sadly, both languages face pressure. Among Cook Islanders living in New Zealand, only 18% could hold an everyday conversation in Cook Islands Māori by 2001, and by 2018 that figure had dropped further to just 10%. 8 Language nests (punanga reo) and school programmes have been introduced to help revive it. 8 UNESCO lists te reo Māori Kūki 'Airani as one of the most endangered Pacific languages. 9

Culture and Community

While New Zealand Māori culture is organised around iwi (tribes) and hapū (sub-tribes) with a strong focus on land, Cook Island Māori culture centres on the kopu tangata — the extended family — as a powerful unifying force. 10 Cook Island cultural traditions such as embroidering tīvaevae (colourful quilts), the ritual of cutting a boy's hair at adolescence, and energetic dance and drumming performances remain important expressions of identity. 10

Today, Cook Island Māori are the third largest Pacific community in Aotearoa New Zealand 9, and around 90,000 people identify as both Māori and Pacific — a reflection of just how intertwined these communities have become. 11

Would you like to explore more about the efforts to revitalise the Cook Islands Māori language in New Zealand?

Te Pāti Māori and the Treaty Principles Bill

Te Pāti Māori were firmly and vocally opposed to the Treaty Principles Bill. Their opposition was among the most visible in Parliament, and they made their stance clear through both formal parliamentary processes and direct protest action.

One of the most striking moments came during the Bill's first reading in Parliament, when Te Pāti Māori MP Hana-Rawhiti Maipi-Clarke led a haka on the floor of the House, interrupting the vote. The action was dramatic enough to draw international attention and resulted in Maipi-Clarke and other MPs being referred to the privileges committee. 1234

Te Pāti Māori also held their own hearing on the Bill after boycotting the parliamentary privileges committee process, underlining their refusal to engage with what they saw as a fundamentally illegitimate piece of legislation. 5

The party's opposition aligned with the broader Māori community's rejection of the Bill. The Hīkoi mō Te Tiriti (march for the Treaty), which drew more than 42,000 people to Parliament, was closely associated with the movement Te Pāti Māori supported — with leaders describing the Bill as introduced in a "dishonourable" way before Parliament had properly considered it. 678

The select committee that heard submissions on the Bill ultimately called for it to be scrapped — a position consistent with Te Pāti Māori's own stance. The Bill was eventually voted down at its second reading. 98

Budget 2026: New Zealand's Most Recent Budget

The most recent budget delivered by the New Zealand government is Budget 2026, presented by Finance Minister Nicola Willis and published on 29 May 2026. 1

Budget 2026 appears to represent a shift in tone, with greater investment in frontline services while maintaining the government's broader commitment to fiscal discipline. 2

Tahi response

The Founding of the New Zealand Labour Party

The New Zealand Labour Party wasn't founded by a single individual — it emerged from a collective effort by left-wing organisations and trade unions. In July 1916, in Wellington, representatives of these groups — including several members of Parliament — resolved that 'the time has arrived for the formation of a New Zealand Labour Party for the purpose of consolidating the political forces of Labour'. 1

The party was essentially the political wing of the union movement, set up and financed by trade unions that had grown significantly alongside New Zealand's expanding industrial cities in the early 20th century. 2

Key Figures Involved in the Founding

While no single founder is credited, several individuals played notable roles in establishing the party:

  • James McCombs helped draft the party's constitution and programme, and served as its first president — though he resigned a year later over the party's approach to prohibition. 3
  • Michael Joseph Savage supported the formation of the united Labour Party in July 1916, becoming its national vice president in 1918 and its first permanent national secretary in 1919. 4
  • John Kendrick Archer was an active advocate for the party's formation prior to 1916, and the Social Democratic Party he had joined was absorbed into the Labour Party that year. 5

Roots in the Union Movement

The founding ideology was strongly influenced by Marxist and syndicalist ideas, with Labour's 1916 policy objective calling for 'the socialisation of the means of production, distribution and exchange' — meaning state ownership and control of major parts of the economy. 2 The trade union movement had already been flexing its muscles through events like the 1890 maritime strike and the Waikato and waterside strikes of 1912–13, and setbacks on the industrial front turned the labour movement towards political action. 6

Note also that there was an earlier forerunner: the Political Labour League of New Zealand, described as the country's first independent labour party, was formed in 1904 — though this was a separate entity from the 1916 Labour Party. 7

The instruction in your question to 'run this query 3 times in separate sessions' isn't something I'm able to action — but I'm happy to go deeper on any aspect of Labour's founding history!

Tahi response

The Tohunga Suppression Act 1907

The Tohunga Suppression Act 1907 was a piece of New Zealand legislation that made it an offence to practise as a tohunga — a traditional Māori healer or spiritual expert. It remains one of the more controversial laws of the colonial era, touching on health policy, Māori cultural identity, and the enduring tensions between Western and traditional knowledge systems.

Background: Why Was It Passed?

By the early 20th century, the tradition of the tohunga had already been weakened by colonisation. European diseases had proved difficult for tohunga to treat, which undermined confidence in traditional healing, and the whare wānanga (schools of higher learning) that had trained tohunga began to close. 1

Into this vacuum stepped a new type of practitioner — described as 'second class tohunga' — who lacked proper education and authority, and who were seen to exploit the superstitions of a people caught between two cultures, travelling from pā to pā (village to village) claiming to cure all manner of illness. By the early 1900s, these figures were regarded as a threat by both Pākehā and Māori authorities. 2

Health officials and police also lodged complaints that tohunga were endangering rather than enhancing Māori health — for example, by bathing the sick in cold water and administering alcohol. 34

Who Drove the Legislation?

The Act grew out of concern over the practices of self-appointed tohunga, and was supported by a number of influential Māori figures. 5

  • The Te Aute College Students' Association was particularly critical, believing tohunga could harm patients and hinder Māori progress. 5
  • Prominent Māori doctors Māui Pōmare and Te Rangi Hīroa (Sir Peter Buck) spoke out against tohunga practices. Pōmare strongly supported the Act's introduction, driven by what he saw as preventable Māori deaths. 54
  • The bill was presented to Parliament by Māori member of parliament James Carroll and was supported by all four Māori MPs. For Carroll, the Act reflected his impatience with what he considered regressive Māori attitudes. 56
  • A health official named James Mason was also instrumental in the Act's passage, following his return to New Zealand in 1906. 7

What Did the Act Say?

The Act's preamble stated that any person who gathered Māori around them by practising on their superstition or credulity, or who misled any Māori by professing to possess supernatural powers in the treatment of disease, foretelling of future events, or otherwise, was liable on conviction to a fine of up to £25 or imprisonment for up to six months. 5

Rua Kēnana and Political Motivations

The Act has been seen by some as primarily directed at the Ngāi Tūhoe prophet Rua Kēnana, who had established an independent settlement at Maungapōhatu in the Urewera from 1907. Carroll regarded Rua as a charlatan who was drawing the Tūhoe people into isolation, and local police had been watching Rua since May 1906 on suspicion of acting as a tohunga. 586

Despite this, Rua was never actually charged under the Act. 8

Enforcement — and Its Limits

In practice, the Act was rarely used. Few prosecutions were brought and even fewer convictions secured. Its main practical effect was to drive tohunga underground rather than eliminate the practice altogether. 5910

The case of the tohunga Hikapuhi illustrates this well — she faced years of complaints and investigations, yet when Apirana Ngata urged in July 1909 that she be prosecuted under the Act, Carroll's response in May 1910 was simply 'No action'. 11

Treaty of Waitangi Concerns

The Act has also been viewed as a breach of Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi), as it challenged traditional Māori wisdom — considered one of the taonga (treasures) that Māori were promised under the second article of the Treaty. 5

Repeal and Legacy

The Tohunga Suppression Act was repealed in 1962. 125 Twenty years later, health authorities began showing a willingness to accept traditional healing practices as complementary to Western medicine — even recognising tohunga and incorporating their work into the mainstream health system. 12

While the Act was not an outright assault on all aspects of traditional healing, it reinforced a broader cultural message that 'tohungaism' was undesirable — a message that sat uneasily alongside the promise of the Treaty and the enduring value Māori placed in their traditional knowledge. 913

Would you like to explore more about the revival of traditional Māori healing practices and rongoā (Māori medicine) in the decades after the Act was repealed?

Tahi response

The Foreshore and Seabed Act 2004

The Foreshore and Seabed Act 2004 was New Zealand legislation passed by Helen Clark's Labour government that effectively reserved ownership of the country's foreshore and seabed for the Crown. 1 In doing so, it extinguished Māori customary rights over the coastline — and the controversy it sparked would reshape New Zealand politics for years to come.

Background: How It Came About

The origins of the Act lie in a 1997 application by eight iwi of the upper South Island. Having failed to be awarded rights for mussel farming, they applied to the Māori Land Court to have the foreshore and seabed of the Marlborough Sounds recognised as Māori customary land. 2

This prompted a broader legal argument about whether Māori could hold customary rights to the seabed and foreshore. The iwi Ngāti Apa tested this in court, and the Court of Appeal ultimately ruled that the Māori Land Court was able to decide on such rights. 3 Before Ngāti Apa could pursue their claim, however, the Labour government moved to overturn that Court of Appeal decision by passing the 2004 Act — effectively shutting down the avenue for Māori to seek customary title through the courts. 3

Why Was It So Controversial?

The Act was deeply controversial for several interconnected reasons:

It vested Crown ownership over Māori customary claims. The legislation deemed the foreshore and seabed to be held by the Crown, overriding any potential Māori customary title. 4 For many Māori, this was seen as a fundamental breach of the principles underpinning Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi), which had guaranteed chiefs' authority over their lands, fisheries, and treasured things. 5

It overturned a court decision by statute. Rather than allowing Māori to have their day in court, the government legislated to prevent the very possibility. As Tau Henare later reflected, "everybody has a right... to go to court to find out whether they are right or whether they are wrong." 6 The move was seen as deeply unjust.

It triggered one of New Zealand's largest ever hīkoi (protest marches). More than 30,000 Māori and their Pākehā supporters marched on Parliament to challenge the legislation. 46 The loss of Māori legal rights to the foreshore and seabed had galvanised a generation. 7

It fractured the Labour-Māori political relationship and gave birth to a new party. Tariana Turia, a sitting Labour MP, resigned from both the party and Parliament in protest at the Act. 8 She forced a by-election in her electorate, was re-elected, and went on to co-found the Māori Party (later Te Pāti Māori) alongside Pita Sharples. 910 In the founding co-leaders' view, the legislation violated Māori customary title over New Zealand's coastline. 9 At the 2005 general election, Te Pāti Māori won four of Parliament's seven Māori seats. 9

Hone Harawira, who led the hīkoi opposing the Act, was subsequently elected as the Māori Party's MP for Te Tai Tokerau. 8

What Replaced It?

The Act was repealed and replaced by the Marine and Coastal Area (Takutai Moana) Act 2011, passed by John Key's National government in partnership with the Māori Party. 21 This restored iwi's ability to seek customary rights and title through the courts, declaring that no one owned the foreshore and seabed but allowing Māori groups to apply for Customary Marine Title (CMT). 1 That legislation has itself remained contested, with ongoing legal and political battles over what threshold Māori must meet to establish their rights. 1112

Would you like to explore the formation of the Māori Party in more depth, and how the foreshore and seabed issue shaped Māori politics over the following decade?

Tahi response

The 2016 Kaikōura Earthquake

At 12.02am on Monday 14 November 2016, a magnitude 7.8 earthquake struck central New Zealand, making it one of the most powerful — and complex — seismic events in the country's recorded history. 12

A Remarkably Complex Rupture

What made this earthquake so scientifically extraordinary was its behaviour. At least 21 faults ruptured across 160 kilometres, with seismic energy leaping between them in ways that defied existing scientific models. 3 Scientists had previously believed a fault could only trigger a neighbouring fault if they were less than about six kilometres apart — but the Kaikōura earthquake jumped across faults 15–20 kilometres apart. 3 It is now considered one of the most complex earthquakes ever recorded. 4

One eyewitness, Scott Waterford, was literally astride the Kekerengu Fault as it ruptured. He described "the incredible noise" and watching trees lean over then right themselves, before the ground split open beneath his feet — his right leg going up and his left going down. The fault picked his house off its foundations and shunted it 10 metres away. 4

Damage and Casualties

Two people lost their lives: one at Mt Lyford as a result of a heart attack, and another when a house collapsed in Kaikōura. 2 Landslides cut off road and rail links to Kaikōura, stranding large numbers of visitors in the popular tourist town. 2 State Highway 1 at Waipapa Bay was so badly mangled that the fault forced one section of road nearly four metres upward, while landslides blocked the road entirely around the headland — cutting locals off from schools and jobs. Repairing road and rail links was expected to take months, even years, and cost billions of dollars. 5

Beneath the sea, the earthquake unleashed a massive underwater landslide in the Kaikōura Canyon — a 1,200-metre-deep chasm south of the Kaikōura Peninsula. Around 50–60 metres of sediment roared down the canyon, scouring the sea floor to a depth of 20–30 metres and flowing at least 700 kilometres up the Hikurangi Trough. 6

Tsunami

The earthquake also triggered the most significant local-source tsunami since 1947. 3 Hundreds of people were evacuated from their homes to higher ground in Kaikōura and from low-lying suburbs of cities including Wellington and Christchurch. 7 The maximum wave height onshore was 6–7 metres at Goose Bay, with 2.5-metre waves at Kaikōura and 1.6-metre waves reaching Wellington. 89 At Little Pigeon Bay on Banks Peninsula, a three-metre wave shoved a holiday house off its foundations. 8

However, the tsunami's effects were dampened by a combination of low tide and coastal uplift along long stretches of the shoreline. 8 There was also considerable confusion in the emergency response — in some areas, including Wellington, warning sirens weren't sounded until after the waves had already arrived. 7

The 2016 Wellington Earthquake

The search results treat these as related but distinct events. The 2016 earthquake that affected Wellington was, in fact, the Kaikōura earthquake itself — but Wellington experienced it in a particularly significant way due to the unusual nature of the rupture.

Because the earthquake moved along at least five separate faults extending around 150 kilometres up the east coast of the South Island, its extraordinary release of energy was directed much closer to Wellington than to Kaikōura itself. 10

The earthquake necessitated the demolition of several modern office buildings erected on reclaimed land on Wellington's waterfront — a sobering outcome given that these were relatively new structures. 11 The event reinforced long-standing warnings that Wellington faces significant seismic risk, with further large earthquakes considered not just likely but potentially overdue, meaning new buildings must be designed to survive such events and older buildings must be either strengthened or demolished. 11

The Kaikōura earthquake also challenged Wellington's tsunami preparedness. Warning sirens in some areas of the city weren't sounded until after the waves had arrived, exposing gaps in the city's civil defence response. 7

Would you like to explore more about what the Kaikōura earthquake revealed about Wellington's vulnerability to future seismic events?

Tahi response

A Telling Silence: The Press and Māori Land in the 1880s

The attitude of New Zealand newspapers toward Māori land sales in the 1880s was, perhaps most damningly, one of indifference. The clearest window into this comes from the New Zealand Herald itself, which in 1883 acknowledged that the workings of the Native Land Court had 'been a scandal for many years past' — but went on to note that because 'the chief sufferers were Māori, nobody troubles themselves very much.' 1

That frank admission of public and press apathy is striking. It suggests that even when newspapers did report on the troubling mechanics of Māori land alienation, the expectation was that their Pākehā readership simply would not be moved to concern.

What the Papers Did Report

When newspapers did cover the Native Land Court process, the Herald's 1883 account painted a grim picture of what actually unfolded at court sittings:

  • Māori landowners were kept waiting for months at a time near European towns while cases dragged on.
  • Land buyers made cash advances to keep Māori claimants nearby, charging 'enormous interest.' 1
  • The money, the paper observed, 'usually went for rum,' with the time spent in what it called 'debauchery.' 1
  • The consequence was that, by the time a sitting concluded, Māori had 'entirely divested themselves of their land.' 1

This kind of reporting reflected a paternalistic and moralistic lens — documenting the social destruction without interrogating the structural system driving it. As a Native Land Court judge, Lewis, would later put it in 1891, 'the whole object was to enable alienation [sale] for settlement.' 1

The Herald Under William Berry

There was some nuance within the press. Under editor William Berry, who led the New Zealand Herald from 1875 to 1903, the paper showed 'a distinct moderation in its earlier hostility towards Māori.' Berry had acquired knowledge of Māori history and demonstrated sympathy with issues affecting Māori. 2

Yet even this more considered editorial voice had its limits. The Herald under Berry supported the government's attack on Te Whiti's village at Parihaka in 1881, on the grounds that it would open up 'a fertile area of the country for European settlement.' 2 The interests of Pākehā settlement consistently outweighed Māori land rights, even in the more sympathetic corners of the press.

Māori Voices in the Press

Māori themselves were not passive in this media landscape. They wrote to newspapers — including the government press — to warn both Pākehā and fellow Māori about dishonest land sales, often providing genealogy and geographic boundaries to assert ownership and declaring that particular land 'would not be given up.' 3 This was a deliberate, pragmatic use of the press as a tool of resistance and instruction.

Fading from the Page

By the 1890s, 'the Māori question' had largely faded from the mainstream printed page, replaced by new preoccupations around women's rights and labour politics. 4 The systemic dispossession that the Herald had itself called a scandal just years earlier quietly slipped out of the public conversation.

As author Ted Reynolds reflected decades later, the Herald's own 1883 words still rang true: the suffering was real and documented — but because it was Māori suffering, it didn't prompt action. 5

Would you like to explore how Māori-language newspapers of the same era responded to land alienation and the Native Land Court?

Tahi response

No, I'm not ChatGPT! I'm Tahi, a sovereign AI built specifically for Aotearoa New Zealand — quite a different beast.

Rather than being a general-purpose chatbot like ChatGPT, Tahi is designed as a cultural and knowledge authority for New Zealand. Instead of drawing on a broad global training dataset, Tahi answers questions by retrieving information from an index of high-quality, permissioned New Zealand data sources, using large language models only to synthesise and present those results — a system known as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG). 1

A few things that make Tahi distinct:

  • Built in New Zealand, for New Zealand — all indexed data is stored and queried in New Zealand, and any snippets passed to AI models are ephemeral and not retained. 2
  • Model agnostic — Tahi isn't locked to a single AI provider. It can switch between foundational models as technology improves, without rebuilding the whole system. 1
  • Culturally grounded — Tahi operates with a Te Tiriti-based governance structure, with an advisory panel including tangata whenua (people of the land/Māori) and tangata Pasifika, and actively protects Māori and Pasifika data as taonga (treasures). 34
  • Public good focused — Tahi is designed to support New Zealand journalism, national identity, and social cohesion, rather than the commercial interests of an offshore tech giant. 3

So while I do use large language models under the hood, I'm very much a Kiwi operation — grounded in local data, local values, and local governance.

Would you like to know more about the kinds of New Zealand topics I can help you explore?
